@@41663830 <h> Focus : A liberal manifesto in a time of inequality and climate change <p> A worker standing near heavy machinery used to dump sand on the shoreline of Miami Beach , Florida . Dozens of trucks have started dumping hundreds of thousands of tons of sand on Miami Beach as part of US government measures to protect Florida 's tourist destinations against the effects of climate change . - AFP <p> KUALA LUMPUR : It 's hard being a liberal these days . The world seems to want to go in the opposite direction . Authoritarianism is on the rise in many countries , mercantilism in the US , populism everywhere from Brazil to the Philippines and South-East Asia , and an oxymoronic " illiberal democracy " in Hungary and Poland . <p> Liberals seem particularly irrelevant in the two biggest debates of our time : about inequality and global warming . The first has left-wing demagogues baying once again to soak the rich in a new round of class warfare . The second has spooked the loony right into denying the problem ( and indeed science ) and the loony left into flight-shaming , SUV-shaming and just @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ or China 's Red Guards in 1966 . <p> What 's become almost inaudible in these two controversies is an older and more dignified voice : liberalism . By this I do n't mean its risible ( and opposite ) caricatures in the US and Europe . <p> In American usage , a liberal is a big-government lefty who sees society through the lens of identity politics . In left-wing European parlance , a liberal ( or , worse , " neoliberal " ) is a free-market fundamentalist . <p> Liberalism is neither . It 's a broad and flexible philosophy that values individual freedom . ( Liber is Latin for free ) . That 's why liberals distrust concentrations of power that might oppress us . Often such power is found in the state , which is why liberals prefer limited government ; other times , it 's found in companies , mafias or mobs , in which case liberals oppose those , with regulation and laws . But liberals are a pragmatic bunch too , rising to the new challenges of each era . <p> The liberal approach @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ grappled at the highest intellectual level with inequality . If you want to think deeply about " justice as fairness , " you could do worse than open the book of that title by John Rawls . If you want the best answer to Rawls , read Robert Nozick , his former colleague and sparring partner down the hallway at Harvard University . <p> What liberals since John Locke , the patriarch of the tradition , have agreed on is that you need property to be free . Invariably , some people will end up with more than others . Rawls would say that 's fine as long as everybody benefits , including the worst off . Nozick would respond that it 's fine even if they do n't , as long as those who get more have earned it legitimately . <p> It 's a mistake , moreover , to look at the distribution of wealth or income only at one point in time . Yesterday 's haves could turn into tomorrow 's have-nots and vice versa , all as a result of life choices , luck , skill @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ people , or the same families across generations , keep ending up at the top or bottom no matter what they do . <p> In a free and fair society , social mobility is therefore more important than the distribution of wealth ; and equality of opportunity trumps equality of outcomes . That 's why some liberals can live with inheritance taxes , which are assessed a single time on an estate to prevent the accumulation of vast riches across generations . By contrast , they frown on wealth taxes , which now seem to be back in fashion . Not only are those intrusive and hard to assess year after year , they also miss the point , which is , as Jeremy Bentham put it in 1795 , to make the poor richer , not the rich poorer . <p> In ensuring equal opportunity , however , there really is a lot of work to be done , at least outside of Scandinavia . That includes assuring equal access to education and health care . In both areas , the US in particular has a problem , which @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of opportunity . <p> A fascinating twist is a new iteration of an old idea called the Universal Basic Income . The state could pay everybody , irrespective of achievement or need , a stipend . On the face of it , you 'd expect liberals to be against that ( and many are ) . Does n't it sound like big government ? And would n't people simply stop working ? <p> But consider the full idea . A basic income would n't complement but completely replace the existing welfare state , as it exists in its full bureaucratic insanity . No more fraud or endless administration , since everybody gets the benefit automatically . A basic income would thus radically simplify , and possibly shrink , the Leviathan . <p> A well designed basic income , moreover , would be too meager to allow recipients to live luxuriously . So you 'd still need to earn your livelihood if it 's to be a good one . But the state-provided cash could get you through periods of sickness or education , including adult retraining as the economy becomes @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ use the key word , free you to fulfill your individual potential . <p> Yet we need empirical data . How would people respond , and would they stop working ? How much would it cost ? Unfortunately , the few places that have conducted experiments have done it wrong . You ca n't , for instance , just pay a few unemployed people , as Finland did , because that 's not a " universal " income . For now , the jury 's out . <p> Liberals have also thought deeply about climate change , long before it even became a threat . It 's just that they 've used different names for that type of problem , such as " market failure " and " the tragedy of the commons . " If individuals use a resource they believe to be infinite because it does n't seem to have a price , they will overuse it until it 's ruined . This explains why people overfish the oceans , or why a public bathroom stinks whereas your one at home does n't . <p> Our atmosphere is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ We keep consuming stuff without heed to the greenhouse gases we and our products emit along the way , because we can not see the cost of the emissions we cause . The liberal answer is therefore to make that hidden cost of carbon visible and force all of us to pay it . <p> There are two ways of doing that , and in principle it does n't matter which you choose . You could set the price of carbon with a tax on it and observe how much that reduces the quantity of emissions . Or you could fix the quantity of emissions by selling allowances , then observe what that does to the price of carbon . This takes the form of an emissions trading system . <p> Several places already have such a system , including the European Union . But until now these markets have n't covered enough sectors of the economy or restricted the allowances enough to make the carbon price really hurt . The biggest promise of the EU 's Green Deal now in the works is that it plans to expand its @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ signal of a much higher carbon price would quickly percolate through the whole economy , influencing all our decisions . Companies would invest in technology to make their factories cleaner . Homeowners may find they can save money by retrofitting their heating systems . Businesspeople may take the train instead of the plane , or may Skype instead of traveling at all . I might even stop skiing , once I factor in the costs of my journey and of the ( regrettably necessary ) snow machines . <p> The advantage of this approach is that all these decisions would still be decentralized - that is , taken by free individuals . Other approaches out there , by contrast , involve different forms of central planning . And those are the bad ideas in Europe 's Green Deal . <p> These include subsidies to homeowners and companies , favoring particular technologies , and rationing , shaming or even banning specific behaviors , such as flying . Government is n't good at deciding , say , whether future cars should run on fuel cells , batteries or something else , or @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ something else . And central bankers certainly have no business steering money to greener uses . All such steps would distort the allocation of capital and deprive people of freedom . <p> Whether the topic is inequality , the climate , trade , migration , data privacy or almost anything else , it 's a pity that liberals and liberal parties do n't have a bigger voice in policy today . As others turn toward collectivism and the politics of resentment , whether it 's nationalism or socialism or green authoritarianism , liberals should be more confident in presenting our philosophy of freedom as the better alternative . It would be a liberalism adapted to the needs of our planet . For want of a better label , let 's call it planetism. - Andreas Kluth ( Bloomberg ) 
@@41663842 <h> The psychological impact of the Australian bushfires <p> The eyes of the world are fixed on the bushfires ravaging Australia and we are following the progress of the Malaysian firefighting , rescue and recovery personnel who have joined teams from other Commonwealth nations . <p> Nearly 30 people have died , 2,000 homes have been incinerated and a quarter of a million residents have been forced to flee the raging flames , so what may be the long-term effects on the refugees and firefighters ? <p> We may think it 's an easy choice to leave your home when your life is in danger , but it 's a life changing decision . You can imagine the pressure you would feel trying to make up your mind , and then when you decide to go , there are even harder choices of what to take with you . Thousands of Australians have suffered the stress of making these awful decisions , but there may be even worse to come because under pressure we tend to make worse decisions . <p> This means that as the immediate stress of leaving begins to subside the first signs of longer-term psychological @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ their choices , questioning whether or not they did the right thing and in some cases remembering something important that they overlooked . The stress of loss will be greatly magnified by the feelings of foolishness or remorse , and these unhappy emotions will continue long after the smoke has cleared . This is why immediate access to counselling and support services is so important and why the Australians have placed such a heavy emphasis on deploying health professionals to all of the refugee centres . <p> Fleeing the flames is stressful enough , but it takes deep courage to walk into the fire . The 60 Malaysian personnel who are now working to beat back the inferno are displaying bravery and discipline of the highest order . <p> Some psychologists view courage almost like a ginger root that will grow back , but people who reach the limit of their endurance may suffer a sudden and complete emotional collapse . The Australian authorities will be aware of these concerns and are undoubtedly monitoring the situation . <p> Hopefully the bushfires will soon begin to subside ; residents and firefighters @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the initial elation at surviving the disaster may be replaced by deep unhappiness as they realise the enormity of it all . <p> Some people may appear to be fine , but we are all much more aware that post-traumatic stress disorder ( PTSD ) may manifest itself months or even years in the future . PTSD can be a silent epidemic , so all of those that have been affected by this disaster will require access to good mental health provision for a very long time to come . <p> One of the most powerful ways to help people heal psychologically is to prevent tragedy repeating itself . Science suggests human-induced climate change has played a major part in these fires and that things will get worse unless there is change . So the best psychological outcome of this tragedy would be for us all to do our part in achieving the United Nations Sustainability Goal on Climate Action . <p> Hew Gill <p> Professor of Psychology &amp; Associate Provost <p> Sunway University <p> We 're sorry , this article is unavailable at the moment . @@41664422 <h> Our global desertification <p> WHAT are deserts ? Geographers define them as regions with less than 250 millimetres of rain a year . <p> Botanists will declare that only specialised vegetation is found in such areas , which will spring into life and bloom when rain occurs . <p> Most of us will agree that they are areas of very low population density -- they are literally deserted by mankind . <p> The last definition is not strictly true , for underneath the veneer of broken rock and sand lie vast oil and natural gas resources in the Middle Eastern and North African countries . <p> Such areas have witnessed warfare in the last and in this very century . <p> Of the total ice-free land on Earth of 126.33 million square km , 48.9 million square km is desert but this excludes ice-sheets and ice-caps , which are classified as deserts and only because very few people live in such areas in sub-zero temperatures . <p> Manmade deserts <p> It has been thought that very likely mankind has created most of the deserts we witness today . <p> If this is so , then we have made @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ uninhabitable ! It is somewhat fallacious to say that all deserts are caused by low rainfall inputs . <p> However , there are three smallish deserts , amounting to 6 per cent of the total desert area , that are not made by humans . <p> All three are in the southern hemisphere : the Atacama ( South America ) , the Namib/Kalahari ( Southwest Africa ) , and the West Australian . <p> These deserts are caused by their very location along the Tropic of Capricorn plus the fact that they are on west side of each continent with three very cold ocean currents bearing Antarctic waters running along their coastlines . <p> These ocean currents are respectively , the Humboldt , the Benguela , and the West Australian . <p> Ninety-four per cent of the world 's deserts cover vast areas of Northern Africa , and Central Asia to include the Sahara , Middle Eastern , Arabian , and Gobi together with those in the Western USA and Central Australia . <p> As opposed to popular misconceptions that they are covered in sand for , in fact , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ fractured rubble . <p> As they stand today , it is almost inconceivable , that these were once vibrant semi-arid ecosystems with vast areas covered in vegetation supporting mammals . <p> More diverse than today 's Kenya 's and Tanzania 's savanna grassland National Parks of Masai Mara and Serengeti , they existed without today 's cattle ranches or indeed tourists ! <p> Man has likely made 33 per cent of potentially habitable land on Earth uninhabitable . <p> What happened to these semi-arid savannas ? <p> We arrived on Earth armed with fire , using it extensively to drive game and so we became the top predators of huge herds of herbivorous mammals populating their natural habitats . <p> From between 200,000 and 100,000 years ago , we moved throughout Africa , constantly firing the fragile savanna grasslands as we migrated . <p> Repeated burning of these areas destroyed many plant seeds that would have survived occasional firings . <p> From Africa we entered Eurasia , via the Sinai Peninsula , and even reached Australia 40,000 years ago . <p> Australian research scientists have dated the first human arrivals @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ now , dry lake-bed sediments . <p> This suggests that we drove out or even hunted animal to include a ' rhinoceros-sized wombat ' ! Without grazing mammals , trees and shrubs , devastated by repeated firings , winds rapidly removed the top soils to be washed by occasional rains down to river deltas . <p> These very delta sediments have been dated at the same age as the layer of soot in those once inland lake beds . <p> As humans advanced into western Asia , they saw vast herds of gazelles and wild asses roaming the Mesopotamian plains with vegetation in abundance . <p> These animals were trapped and slaughtered almost to extinction . <p> Fire was again the driving force , followed by soil erosion and vegetative degradation . <p> ' A scorched earth ' policy has often been used in warfare but today guerrilla warfare has the same effect in driving people from their homes and livelihoods particularly in semi-arid environments . <p> Today , we see this in northern Kenya , Chad , Burkina Faso , and the Yemen where creeping desert takes over once @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ often used by farmers rings true there , " If you look after the land , the land will look after you . " <p> Yemeni refugees sit near tents in a desert camp . -- AFP file photo <p> Urban deserts <p> In our so called ' search for progress ' , we face a situation today with the push and pull factors of rural to urban migration . <p> The perceived financial gains through greater opportunities appear to lie in cities which , in most parts of our world , are no more than concrete and tarmac deserts housing forever increasing population densities . <p> In 2018 , The World Bank reported that of our global population , in that year , 55 per cent lived in urban areas . <p> In the UK , 81 per cent live in such areas and Malaysia is just short of this figure at 76 per cent . <p> Whilst limits to urban sprawl are declared by city planners , ever increasing numbers of urban dwellers see ever increasingly ' gnawing ' at the countryside on the edges of towns of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ made from their land sales to potential urban developers causing yet further city growth ! <p> In some cities , shanty towns develop on steep hillsides where ' houses ' are built of any materials that the ' newcomers ' can salvage from wherever . <p> With no main drainage and no refuse disposal facilities , as these folk pay no land rent or council taxes , what hope have these people have other than casual labour if lucky ? While they eke out a living and survive in relative squalor , the favela or shanty town dwellers are today 's lost people in our world , ignored by both national and local governments until landslides or earthquakes occur and consciences are pricked . <p> Amongst the chaotic planning in some city-developments , there is hope in incorporating green areas into our urban structures , as seen both in Kuching and London , in parks , natural forest/woodland areas , replanting schemes , and tree preservation orders . <p> It is interesting that a very recent study of school sites in the UK revealed that those schools which have hedges @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ emitted by traffic on nearby roads . <p> As climate change bites more deeply and population growth spirals , so our once natural environment , which we first inherited , is forever decreasing through increased desiccation and urbanisation . <p> Certainly , we all need somewhere to live but , alas , as our habitable land area forever shrinks , we need our scientific researchers to provide us with answers and moreover governments worldwide to listen to and respond accordingly to their solutions , however temporary . <p> Remission is one thing in life , a miracle or complete cure is another long term objective that , unfortunately , can not be attained overnight . <p> As the old proverb says , " Where there is a will , there is a way " , but we should not allow our materialistic gains to overrule our hearts and minds . 
@@41664717 <h> Mahathir highlights Malaysia 's conservation efforts <p> Bernama <p> Published 27 Sep 2019 , 7:47 am <p> Updated 27 Sep 2019 , 11:24 am <p> Advertisement <p> Dr Mahathir Mohamad said Malaysia takes its commitment to conservation seriously and is proud to be a signatory to an extensive list of global treaties on conservation , wildlife , forestry and the environment . <p> The prime minister said the country also viewed multilateral environmental agreements as a key part of its formula for balance in the sense of its responsibility to the planet and its responsibility to its people to ensure their right to food , clothing and shelter . <p> He pointed out that Malaysia also had kept to its environmental promises , including the one made at the Rio Earth Summit in 1992 where it committed to maintaining at least 50 percent of its landmass under forest cover . <p> " Today , almost three decades later , I am proud to announce that we have not reneged on that pledge . Malaysia 's forest cover is currently at 55.3 percent or 18.3 million hectares of our total land area , exceeding Malaysia 's initial commitment at @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ nation 's will and commitment to conserve and sustainably manage our forest , its flora and fauna , " he said at ' Environmental Stewardship in Addressing Poverty to Achieve Sustainable Development for All ' , a side event of the United Nations General Assembly held in New York yesterday . <p> Malaysia was the main organiser of this event , with support from Pakistan , Turkey and the United Nations Department of Economic and Social Affairs . <p> Mahathir said despite Malaysia 's economic growth and rapid urbanisation , maintaining its forests and making existing agricultural land more productive to meet increasing demand could not have been achieved without adopting pragmatic , progressive and sustainable development policies . <p> He nonetheless noted that although Malaysia had put in place various efforts to prevent the environmental loss , it still faced challenges from its growing socio-economic development needs . <p> " The increasing population of Malaysia brings with it an increased demand for food , water and other infrastructure which places pressure on our natural resources and environment , " he said . <p> Mahathir also highlighted Malaysia 's Eleventh @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ been allocated to implement a wide range of actions to address climate change as well as to further enhance conservation and restoration efforts .

According @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ rakyat has taken quite a knock : the " satisfaction index " towards Dr Mahathir Mohamad has dropped ; the people 's " happiness " with the federal government has also declined . The decline among Malays was down to about 50% . <p> Thus even if PH wins the upcoming Sandakan by-election , an urban seat , there is some cause for alarm . <p> In his interview with Channel News Asia in Singapore recently , Anwar Ibrahim claimed that the PH government had faced a backlash from the majority Malay community because it had succumbed too much to " pressure from the urban elite and civil society " . <p> The PH government , he said , must do more to focus on the " bread and butter issues faced by the rural Malay population " , such as the low price of rubber . <p> He acknowledged that human rights , judicial independence -- and one could add , ratification of the International Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Racial Discrimination , participation in the Rome Statute of the International Criminal Court and meritocracy -- @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Nonetheless , for Anwar , " the crux of the problem is still the economy . Economic hardship is real , we will have to deal with this with the greatest urgency , or risk a rise in religious bigotry and racism " . <p> No doubt , Umno and Pas have been working together and pushing the race and religion agenda in a bid to win votes from rural Malays in the past few by-elections . <p> Consequently , the rural Malays , Anwar argued , have wrongly perceived that the PH government has been formulating policies that marginalise them . <h> Urban elite and rural poor divide ? <p> That said , Anwar also believed that " the urban elite sets a list of priorities which are a disconnect from the real problems of the poor , and at times , the elite seems to ignore these real problems ... I have not heard them talking about ... poverty , inequality ... " , said Anwar in the interview . <p> He called upon the urban folk to go down to the rural districts , engage with the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " are concerned about their welfare . <p> In this regard , he stressed the need to continue the affirmative action policy . He said the government would continue to help the rural poor , including helping them to get into the universities , so that they could be brought up to the same level as the rest . <p> Anwar 's remarks must be in response to the negative reporting that has been carried especially in the Chinese media regarding the PH government 's announcement to raise the intake of students into the matriculation programme from 25,000 to 40,000 students , of whom 90% would be bumiputera . <p> Significantly , Anwar also stressed in the interview that " the government 's economic agenda must be needs-based and not race-based and racial politics must be shed for the country to move forward " . <p> If that be the case , the PH must ensure that those additional bumiputera recruited into matriculation classes , with an eye towards helping them to enrol in public universities subsequently , come from the bottom 40% of households ( B40 ) . The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ BR1M into Bantuan Sara Hidup. which is rightly targeting the B40 . <p> Alas , two other PH policies -- raising the minimum wage by a mere RM50 from RM1,050 to RM1,100 and the health insurance scheme mySalam National Protection Scheme , which many have criticised will benefit an insurance company rather than the B40 for whom the scheme is aimed at -- are inconsistent with what Anwar is advocating . <p> Perhaps we can access some ideas on how to reach down to the B40 from former Sungai Siput MP Dr Jeyakumar Devaraj , who has elaborated on the plight of the poor in Malaysia in an article published in the Aliran website on 2 March 2019 and deserves to be read , if you have not already done so . <p> Entitled " PH 's survival hinges on whether it can ease poverty " , Devaraj lists some of these poor communities in Malaysia , among them the " urban pioneers " , who opened the fringes of our urban areas but who have continued to live in squatter tenements ; those who live in run-down flats which @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in plantation communities ; and part-time plantation workers whose livelihoods are threatened by current low commodity prices for the rubber or palm oil they produce . @@41665443 <h> New Malaysia not dead -- but it needs a jumpstart <p> It is time for us in Malaysia to counter divisive ethno-religious rhetoric and come together to face up to the problems in the country , writes Anil Netto . <p> It was less than a year ago that many Malaysians felt so upbeat when the general election heralded in a new Malaysia . Such damaging rhetoric has to be properly managed . All this adds up to billions and billions of ringgit . Such projects do little to improve the quality of life of ordinary people struggling to cope with the higher cost of living . <p> What we should be focusing on are the needs of the ordinary people and what they need most : improved schools and hospitals , genuinely affordable homes , efficient public transport and affordable nutritious food . <p> The government also needs to drop the mySalam health insurance scheme especially @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ has to stop focusing on medical tourism , which only aggravates the brain drain from the public sector to the private sector . <p> Instead steps must be taken to immediately improve conditions at the general hospitals where staff struggle to cope with the large crowds almost everyday and more and more beds are crammed into existing wards . <p> Many ordinary folks frequent the crowded general hospitals . This crisis continues to receive scant attention within Malaysia . It is as if we think we will somehow be immune from the fallout . <p> But it is the young people all over the world who will face the full brunt of climate change in a decade or two . And they are mobilising . The Youth Strikes for Climate movement expects to see over 500 events across more than 50 countries around the world . <p> " We , the young , have started to move . " <p> It is time for us in Malaysia to discard and counter divisive ethno-religious rhetoric and come together to face up to the problems in the country , including the need to build climate change resilience . <p> The new Malaysia project is not dead . @@41667829 <h> M'sia returns 150 containers , 3,737 metric tonnes of plastic waste <p> Malaysia has returned 150 containers of plastic waste weighing 3,737 metric tonnes to the countries from which these were imported , at no cost . <p> At a site visit in Butterworth today , Energy , Science , Technology , Environment and Climate Change Minister Yeo Bee Yin said the government is firm in combating the illegal movement of plastic wastes into Malaysia by stopping the sources . <p> " To date , the ministry , through the Department of Environment ( DOE ) has repatriated 150 containers , with approximately 3,737 metric tonnes of plastic wastes that were illegally brought into Malaysia , " Yeo said during a site visit in Butterworth , Penang . <p> " The repatriation exercise does not have any cost implication to the government . The costs were either borne by the exporters or by the shipping liners . This is an unprecedented move by Malaysia , " she added . <p> Yeo said through inspections at the ports and data from the Royal Malaysian Customs Department , most of the containers with the plastic wastes were exported from developed @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ States , Canada , Spain and Japan . <p> Yeo ( centre , below ) vowed to continue with the joint enforcement activities to weed out the illegal import of plastic wastes . <p> It is learnt the Energy Ministry will launch the National Action Plan for Import of Plastic Wastes in February this year . <p> The plan is a guide to all relevant enforcement agencies to strengthen and standardise enforcement activities and procedures to combat the illegal transboundary movement of plastic wastes . <p> " As a party to the Basel Convention , Malaysia also imposes strict requirements in line with Article 4 ( 11 ) of the convention for all imports of mixed plastic wastes to ensure that any transboundary movement of such wastes is conducted in a manner to protect human health and the environment , " Yeo said . <p> She said this during her visit to the Penang Port , organised by the Department of Environment ( DOE ) with the collaboration of enforcement agencies , including the Customs Department and Solid Waste Management and Public Cleansing Corporation Malaysia ( SW Corp ) . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and Sarawak . <p> " The Malaysian government is serious about combating the import of illegal wastes as we do not want to be the garbage bin of the world , " Yeo said . 
@@41668071 <p> ON the 11th of December last year , the government of India issued an amendment to its Citizenship Amendment Act ( CAA ) . <p> The idea behind it was to fast-track citizenship for oppressed minorities in its neighbouring countries -- Pakistan , Afghanistan , and Bangladesh . The groups included are Hindus , Jains , Parsis , Sikhs , and Christians , deliberately excluding Muslims . <p> India also intends on implementing a nation-wide National Registration of Citizens ( NRC ) , which has already been done in the state of Assam . <p> The NRC requires each citizen to provide certain documents on the basis of which a registry is created . It required people to prove that they had an ancestor in India who was born before 1971 . Just like that , the status 1.9 million people became questionable . <p> Out of those , the Muslims will not get citizenship through the CAA . Much to their chagrin , this was not the case . <p> India is witnessing a mass movement in the form of protests against the CAA and NRC . Police barricades have been put up and their entire operation could be shut down at any given point . <p> This is not to say that the women of Shaheen Bagh are not angry , but the softness and love with which they are channelizing is so rare . @@41668077 <p> GEORGE TOWN : Sahabat Alam Malaysia ( SAM ) has called for intensified and increased integration of efforts on sustainable management of the country 's water resources by both the federal and state authorities . <p> This follows news on the sharp drop of water level of dams in Penang and Kedah over the last two months . <p> SAM president Meenakshi Raman said the simplest first steps that could be undertaken by the authorities were to expand on conservation measures at water sources . <p> This could be done through urgent gazetting of the water catchment forests to legally protect them , and reducing the non-revenue water ( NRW ) . <p> She said efforts by agencies such as the National Water Services Commission ( SPAN ) must be amply supported to further enhance protection of water resources and to ensure good water quality . <p> " We have had enough warnings about the precarious and vulnerable situation of our water resources . <p> " We must therefore act before it 's too late and the situation gets too dangerous , more so at this juncture when the impacts of climate change have begun to make their @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ said SAM is greatly alarmed by news on the sharp drop of water level at dams in Penang and Kedah over the last two months . <p> The water level capacity at the Air Itam and Teluk Bahang dams on Penang island currently stood at 62 and 39 per cent . <p> Meanwhile in Kedah , the capacity of its Ahning , Pedu , Muda , Beris and Malut dams were at 62 , 48 , 18 , 81 and 86 per cent , respectively . <p> According to the Penang Water Supply Corporation ( PBAPP ) , between September and December last year , the total rainfall recorded in the Air Itam dam area was only 702mm , equivalent to only 44 per cent of the three year average of 1,577mm , recorded during the same period between 2016 and 2018 . <p> For the Teluk Bahang dam , the total rainfall recorded in the dam area between September and December last year was 1,732 mm , equivalent to only 56 per cent of the same three-year average of 3,101mm . <p> Meenakshi said SAM agreed with the assessment @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ low rainfall in Penang in the last four months of 2019 was linked to climate change . <p> " Today , we have to accept that climate change is the new normal . In light of this human-made global disaster , a more integrated approach to water resource management can no longer be treated lightly by the federal and state governments . <p> " While we laud the call for consumers not to waste water , to address the reality of this new normal , appropriate policy and governance play a central role . <p> " The simplest and most cost-effective step that must be urgently undertaken today is certainly natural conservation at the source . We agree with the chair of SPAN , Charles Santiago , that water catchment areas are fully gazetted so that the ecology of the areas is not compromised , " she said . <p> In Peninsular Malaysia , subsection 10(1) of the National Forestry Act 1984 provides states with the power to gazette Water Catchment Forests within the Permanent Reserved Forest ( PRF ) . <p> Meenakshi , however , said based on the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ only 734,731 hectares of the PRF had been gazetted as Water Catchment Forests . <p> " The total size of the PRF back then and up until 2018 , still stands at 4.8 million hectares , " she added . <p> She said currently , three million hectares of the PRF had been reserved for production activities such as logging and monoculture plantations . <p> Secondly , Meenakshi noted that there was an urgent need for Malaysia to put a stop to its high annual rates of NRW , which is the amount of treated water that flows into a supply system but fails to bring in revenue . <p> This is water that has been lost to piping leakages , system pressure , metering and billing inefficiencies and theft . <p> " For more than two decades , it has been the norm for our annual national NRW rates to not be reduced to less than 30 per cent , despite millions that had been spent . <p> " According to SPAN , the rate of our NRW in 2017 still stood at 35 per cent , which to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ better at keeping its NRW figure lower than the national average at 22 per cent , Kedah lost almost half of its water during this year . <p> " In fact , the five worst NRW rates in the country in 2017 were held by Kedah ( 48 per cent ) , Kelantan ( 49 per cent ) , Pahang ( 48 per cent ) , Perlis ( 63 per cent ) and Sabah ( 54 per cent ) . <p> " This current state of affairs is simply unacceptable , and in fact , downright dangerous , considering our future circumstances , " she added .

@@41670429 <h> Climate change : The future is here <p> We urgently need to get to zero carbon emissions and embark on ambitious drawdown activities , if we are to offer hope to the next generation , writes Sonia Randhawa . <p> There are times when I feel I 've been cheated . <p> By now , yes , we should have flying cars ; we should have at least interplanetary travel ; and we should have hoverboards . Instead , we have increasing inequality and a hopelessness about the future . One of the most important legacies my parents gave me was a belief that my life would be better than theirs and that my children 's lives would be better yet . <p> Obviously , this optimism has never been universal . For indigenous people , whether the Aboriginal peoples of Australia or the Orang Asli of Peninsular Malaysia , the trend has been one of increasing alienation of land and culture . And it was mostly a purely material vision rooted in the post-war experiences of hunger , insecurity and lack . <p> Yet , optimism has driven so much of human culture . It was the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ place for themselves that proved deadly for so many Native Americans at the turn of the last century , when their cultural competence was no longer valued and they could not see how they could pass on the meaning of their lives and their world to future generations . <p> Support the struggle to build a New Malaysia based on Justice , Freedom , Solidarity : <p> It would be huge arrogance to imagine that I , as part of the internationally mainstream , dominant culture , could feel alienation in the same way . And yet , I find it increasingly difficult to give my children that hope and optimism that has been such an important part of my life . <p> Instead , we are bequeathing our children a world of fire and smoke . Last year , my pregnant sister and her son were breathing in the smoke from fires in Indonesia . This year , my family is breathing in the smoke from fires across Australia , smoke that is circumnavigating the globe and due back in Western Australia around a week after it left these @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ at a landfill in Kedah in northern Malaysia , where rice farmers are already reeling from a drought . <p> What 's worse , the fires of 2019 , whether in the Arctic and Siberia , the Amazon , Indonesia or Australia -- intensified by the global heating caused by the increased carbon dioxide in the atmosphere -- are visible feedback loops . As the fires burn , they release more carbon dioxide and other greenhouse gases into the atmosphere , increasing global heating and increasing the chance of fires in the future . These fires are a taste of what will come . <p> In Australia , the visions have been apocalyptic . Darkness spreading at dawn as the smoke from the fires blanketed towns . A 19-year-old woman dying from asthma in her bed , prolonged exposure to the dust and smoke the most likely culprit . Over a billion animals dead , the survivors at risk of starvation in a landscape scorched bare . <p> The land that has burned has included acres upon acres of farmland -- land that produces the food that the cities rely @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of food insecurity , the rising prices of fresh fruit and vegetables are going to put healthy food out of reach of the poorest in society . <p> The smoke is already taking its toll on the young . There have been stories shared on both social and mass media about miscarriages and premature births , possibly linked to the smoke from the fires . <p> In these circumstances , the need for concerted political action has become more visible . A world of 1.5C warming would see even more of these catastrophic fires : It is unlikely that a world of 1.5C warming would stay there . We are already outside any safe climate zone ; there is no carbon budget . <p> We need to urgently get to zero carbon emissions and embark on ambitious drawdown activities . In these , Malaysia has a natural advantage . The warm , wet climate is ideal for growing things , the oldest and most important form of wealth . Malaysia is already at the forefront of the solar industry ; it can also be at the forefront of drawdown . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ future to our children , we need to be exploring these initiatives . But we can not do this while our politicians focus on the chimera of economic growth and increasing exploitation of nature . We need concerted political change , concerted political effort -- and then , perhaps , our children will be able to offer their children the most precious gift of all : hope . <p> ( Visited 139 times , 1 visits today ) <p> Thanks for dropping by ! @@41671525 <h> World risks ' disaster ' as reuse of natural resources declines <p> KUALA LUMPUR , Jan 21 -- The proportion of raw materials the world is reusing has fallen , researchers said today , warning of a " global disaster " as annual consumption of natural resources rose to 100 billion tonnes for the first time . <p> Just 8.6% of the 100 billion tonnes of materials -- including minerals , metals , fossil fuels and biomass -- was put back into service in 2017 , said a report by Amsterdam-based social enterprise Circle Economy , using the latest available data . <p> That compares with 9.1% of materials that were used again two years earlier , when annual consumption was 93 billion tonnes , CEO Harald Friedl told the Thomson Reuters Foundation . <p> " For the first time in history , more than 100 billion tonnes of materials are entering the global economy every year , " he said . <p> " We are going from bad to worse in terms of circularity . This is really dramatic news at the beginning of this year , which only points to one thing : Action . " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ climate change in check , economies should seek to become " circular " by reusing and recycling products , green groups say . <p> The amount of materials the world uses has tripled since 1970 and could double again by 2050 if no action is taken , the United Nations estimates . <p> Friedl said the world was predominantly relying on extracting virgin materials to fuel growth , rather than making better use of existing resources . <p> " We are risking global disaster if we continue this way of limitlessly using the world 's resources , " he said , urging faster action to keep the average rise in global temperatures to limits adopted in the 2015 Paris Agreement . <p> Governments must lead the change in their economies , he said , by collaborating on circular strategies , working with businesses , green groups and academics , and creating national roadmaps . <p> So far , 13 European countries have adopted such roadmaps and in 2019 Colombia became the first Latin American country to launch a similar policy , the report said . <p> Governments , researchers and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ things like supply chains , emissions and materials extraction , said Friedl . <p> That would make it easier to identify opportunities and help economies shift to a more sustainable model , he said by phone from the World Economic Forum meeting in Davos , Switzerland . <p> A global coalition in support of circular economies was also needed , as today 's voluntary and often fragmented efforts could not drive transition fast enough , he added . <p> Cristianne Close , a global markets expert at green group WWF , said economic and financial systems promoted unsustainable consumption and were degrading the natural environment . <p> " The circular economy provides a tangible framework for reducing our impacts , protecting ecosystems and living within the means of one planet , " she added in a statement . -- Thomson Reuters Foundation 
@@41673324 <h> 10 reasons why it can not be business as usual <p> With household debt and federal government debt indicators rising and natural resources being depleted , it can not be business as usual for the Malaysian economy . Anil Netto says it is time for a radical re-think of the economy . <p> The Malaysian economy has entered a critical phase . Integrated into the global trading system as part of an export-led economy and driven by foreign direct investment , the economy today is feeling the effects of a global slowdown . <p> Compounding the problem are other structural weaknesses in the BN government 's economic model that tell us that it simply can not be business as usual for much longer . <p> Unfortunately , the mainstream economic framework does not have an integrated world-view that encompasses a broad range of inter-connected issues . The framework does not look at how economic policy is contributing to widening income inequalities , indebtedness ( individual and national ) , the weakening position of labour , sustainable development and climate change . <p> Support the struggle to build a New Malaysia based on Justice , Freedom , Solidarity : @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ analysis . Rather it brings together some of the fundamental issues affecting us all and calls for them to be adequately integrated into mainstream economic planning . <p> 1 . Massive corruption and rent-seeking <p> This is sapping the life-blood out of the domestic economy . Many large projects appear to have an element of rent-seeking . And when it involves government-related projects , the cronyism can be blatant . <p> This is closely related to the phenomenon of ' money politics ' -- a euphemism for the institutionalised intertwining of business and political interests in the country . Large contracts are often awarded to well-connected companies , whose substantial shareholders invariably include figures who are politically well-connected . Look no farther than ' Cowgate ' . <p> Or in some cases , the contracts or concessionaires may be awarded to major industry players , who become the substantial shareholders in a company holding the concession . But obscure or ' shell ' companies may invariably hold the minority interests in the concessionaire company . The shareholders of these obscure companies tend to be unheard of individuals , raising suspicions @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> 2 . Neoliberal policies <p> The ' Father of Privatisation ' in Malaysia , former premier Mahathir Mohamad introduced Thatcherite policies in Malaysia as state-owned assets were privatised to private companies , many of them crony companies or linked to politically well-connected individuals . <p> Because some of these were essentially monopolies , they reaped super profits that served to enrich the ruling political-business elite . <p> Having lowered corporate tax and income tax ( for the higher bands ) over the years and with its expenditure now spiralling , the government now finds itself faced with persistent fiscal deficits . <p> It is thus forced to reduce subsidies and find new ways of raising funds . So now the public is faced with the prospect of GST , electricity tariff hikes , and 1Care . <p> Even the acquisition and installation of traffic cameras is reportedly being privatisated in a move that could rake even more money for the government -- and certain companies . <p> State-run essential services -- especially health care and education -- are stuck in a rut and their quality has suffered . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ just the rich , are forced to turn to the private sector , where they have to pay higher tariffs . <p> 3 . Trickle down or gush up ? <p> Ever since Malaysia embarked on export-driven investment and achieved impressive GDP growth figures ( except for cyclical slumps or recessionary periods ) , the theory fed to us is that somehow the increase in the GDP would filter down to the rest of the population . <p> For a while that happily seem to be the case , but then real wages found it hard to catch up with productivity and have remained relatively stagnant . <p> As Big Capital sought larger and larger profits and engaged in increasingly speculative activity to park excess liquidity , profits became increasingly concentrated in the top 10 per cent . <p> The growing income divide is not just limited to Malaysia , it is also happening in other economies . The author-activist Arundhati Roy of India put it succinctly when she observed that this was not really a ' trickle down ' of wealth , but a ' gush up ' . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ cent and the bottom 40 per cent grew wider after the Mahathir administration embraced privatisation and other neoliberal policies from the late 1980s ( see Box 1 ) . <p> Box 1 <p> income inequality widens over the years <p> Wages for the working class have been suppressed through a policy of relying on migrant workers ( with labour recruitment agencies raking in huge fees ) . <p> Aggravating this problem is the ' casualisation of labour ' otherwise known by the euphemism of ' labour flexibility ' . Essentially this is a policy that leaves workers with little protection under the law even as large companies resort to labour contractors or labour outsourcing to supply their labour needs . You can only imagine what sort of terms of employment these workers have under their labour contractor bosses . <p> With the Malaysian domestic market being prised opened by free trade agreements that will allow more MNCs to set up shop in Malaysia , what hope is there for labour rights and distributive justice in Malaysia ? <p> 4 . Central government debt <p> The Malaysian public sector debt level @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of GDP just short of the 55 per cent threshold level for concern . And we have been posting persistent fiscal deficits for years now -- no thanks to neoliberal policies , corruption and wasteful spending . How long can we go on like this ? ( See the cover story ' Towards a bankrupt Malaysia ? ' in Aliran Monthly Vol 31 Issue 10 by Dr Subramaniam Pillay . ) <p> 5 . Debt-driven demand <p> The low-wage policy has in turn left many struggling to make ends meet . Not surprisingly , many have turned to borrowing for their various needs , pushing up the household debt to GDP ratio in the last few years ( see Box 2 ) . <p> Box 2 <p> The main components of household debt in Malaysia are housing loans ( 49% ) , car loans ( 27% ) , loans for personal use ( 9% ) , loans to buy securities ( 8% ) , and credit card debt ( 6% ) . <p> While our household debt as a percentage of GDP of 78 per cent may not be exceptionally @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ per cent is worrying ( see Box 3 ) . <p> Sources : Penang Monthly ; OECD Factbook 2010 as published in Finfacts Ireland ; CAP as published in The Malaysian Insider . <p> Now you know why many Malaysians do n't feel so well off despite years of GDP growth , with this level of household debt . <p> About half our disposal income goes to servicing debt . <p> What are some of the factors driving the high household debt/disposal income ratio ? <p> High growth in consumer-driven spending <p> Low interest rates encourage borrowing <p> Easy credit by banking institutions due to financial liberalisation ( translating into fat profits for the banks ? ! ) <p> Rising house prices especially in KL and Penang where the average house price to average income ratios are much higher , and of course , <p> low income/wages <p> 6 . It only measures the rate of growth in the production of goods and services . It ignores the bads -- the environmental costs and the way the wealth is distributed to workers and other ordinary Malaysians ( See accompanying article on ' The Problem with GDP ' ) . <p> 8 . Resource depletion and environmental degradation <p> Natural gas @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ new discoveries are made , oil production is expected to gradually decline in the coming years . <p> The problem is that oil revenue reportedly makes up around 45 per cent of the government budget . And this could spell trouble for federal government debt . <p> It will lead to higher fuel prices for Malaysians . Our dependence on private motor vehicles will aggravate the burden while the massive investment in ( tolled ) highways ( and bridges ) will leave us ill-prepared to deal with a scenario of higher oil prices . <p> As for natural gas , Malaysia may be the third largest exporter in the world , but domestic reserves are only expected to last until 2025 ( although this could be stretched by a few years with new technology squeezing out more ) . Already supplies for domestic power producers have been constrained for whatever reason . ( See Box 4 at the end of this article . ) <p> Our pristine rainforests are rapidly disappearing . Look at Sarawak to see how the primary rainforests have been raped through the indiscriminate granting of timber @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ are becoming increasingly polluted , affecting the catch of fisher folk and depriving the people of fish at affordable prices . <p> 9 . Financialisation and speculation driving up prices <p> Property-centric development , low bank interest rates , easy credit and speculation have driven up house prices . High house prices in turn have an inflationary effect on other essential goods while wages simply can not keep pace . <p> One way out of this would be to spread residential and work areas out of already heavily congested urban areas and introduce a better network of public transport . Tighter controls should be introduced to prevent foreign speculation in domestic property and to tighten up bank loans . New rules should be formulated to ensure property can only be sold after construction is completed . <p> In this way , inflationary pressure on urban land areas and property would be reduced . <p> 10 . Vegetable production in Malaysia is 800,000 tonnes per year -- well short of the domestic requirement of 1.1m tonnes annually . No wonder vegetable prices have been soaring , further burdening the poor Malaysian worker . <p> In view of climate change and rising food prices , we need to reduce our dependence on food imports . <p> But corporate agriculture is not the way to go . It disempowers farmers and promotes the use of chemical fertilisers and hazardous pesticides . Corporate monoculture reduces biodiversity and depletes the soil of nutrients . <p> To narrow the income divide , we need to have a progressive taxation policy . Neoliberal policies should be reversed , and massive investment in essential public sector services is needed . The business of labour subcontractors must stop and workers ' rights upheld . <p> It is no longer enough just to think of the economy in terms of FDI and GDP growth . We need to integrate our approach to the economy so that it considers quality of life , the position of workers , conservation of natural resources , reduction of pollution , and sustainable development . It is Time to CHANGE the Regime @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ I have read and understood it correctly , his main observation is that our education system has deteriorated to the extent that teachers are no longer educating their charges to be individuals with wholesome characters but merely coaching them to pass examinations . <p> From what little experience I have , an event does not normally happen in isolation . It happens nearly always as a result of a series of seemingly insignificant events or factors acting either together or as a result of a chain reaction . Thus the situation of our education system as it is now could have been the result of a combination of factors contributing to make it what it is today . <p> A number of writers have commented on this topic . Their commentaries touched on the need to revamp the syllabus for each subject taught in schools and to learn from other countries the method of teaching , the marking of examinations answer scripts and more . But in all their commentaries so far , none touched on a silent factor : the salary scheme for teachers and heads of schools . <p> Support the struggle to build a @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> If one looks back at the history of the education service , teachers and heads were put on different salary schemes at various times . Among these salary schemes were the Benham scheme , the Unified Teaching Service scheme , the Aziz scheme , the Sistem Saraan Baru ( SSB ) and later , Sistem Saraan Malaysia and today I am told they are on the Skim Perkhidmatan Bersepadu . <p> The SSB or New Remuneration Scheme was introduced in 1992 during Dr Mahathir Mohamad 's first term as Prime Minister . It was a more complex form -- a matrix with several segments , each of which had a number of layers or lines of scale to cater for the various categories of teachers . Each line of the scale allowed a relatively simple progression for a short period of time before reaching its end . Then a teacher would have to be strongly recommended to be promoted to the next higher level . <p> When it was introduced , its selling point was that , because it had so many more segments , it offered teachers more @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . The laggards would theoretically be self-motivated to work harder and improve their performance to be recommended for promotion . The scheme also offered teachers who produced consistently outstanding results in their subjects the chance to become " expert " or " master " teachers with attendant promotions and allowances . <p> But when the scheme was implemented , school heads in their submission of the annual assessment of their staff could only recommend a very limited number of teachers for promotion each year . In fact , only 5% of the 960,000 civil servants qualified for " melintang " ( horizontal ) or " menegak " ( vertical ) increments each year . This resulted in a large number of hardworking ( but not outstanding ) teachers being held back to await their turn the next time around . <p> Even then , I was told , there would be no guarantee that they would be duly recommended and promoted . The result : never before in the history of the education service had so many been punished so much for the sins of so few ! <p> Thus @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ crook to convince their heads they deserved to be promoted . Some teachers discouraged pupils known and proven to be very weak in their subjects not to offer the subjects they taught in the examinations , if possible . In this way , these teachers could produce a respectably high percentage of passes in the subjects they taught . <p> Other teachers resorted to drilling their students on how to answer the questions in the format required in the expected marking schemes of their subjects . This resulted at times in correct answers from students being penalised because their method of working did not conform to that in the marking scheme . In some subjects , a factually correct answer was not accepted " because it 's not in the marking scheme " ! <p> All ideals about character- building and nurturing whatever other worthy qualities of a pupil 's character were secondary . <p> Those teaching the weaker classes , which had students who could not even write their names properly and had no hope of achieving excellent results , would try to get their head 's recommendation in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the salary scheme : Sayang Sayang Boss and Saya Suka Bodek ! <p> Those teachers who had been passed over for promotion year in and year out would either be demoralised , resigned to their fate , or stop putting in their full effort and instead just mark time . <p> Some of these teachers turned to moonlighting as sales agents for insurance or household goods and cosmetics . A few even became part-time tour guides . Many took up tutoring after school hours to earn back the money they deemed they had lost because they had not been promoted . <p> Not surprisingly , a good many of these teachers found their part-time income more lucrative than expected and elected to spend time on their now no-longer-part-time work . <p> Had the period of the SSB , which ended in 2002 , then contributed in no small measure to the state of our education system to the level it is today ? Is there any lesson to be learnt from it ? <p> Khong Kah Yeong is an Aliran newsletter subscriber who has some acquaintance with Malaysian schools . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> Thanks for dropping by ! @@41674895 <h> High alert for new Australia bushfires as heat and winds pick up <p> Unprecedented bushfires fuelled by climate change have killed at least 29 people and devastated vast swathes of the country since September . -- AFP photo <p> Australian firefighters are on high alert for the possibility that scorching temperatures and strong winds could whip up new bushfires on Thursday , coming after rain brought welcome respite to fire-ravaged areas . <p> Unprecedented bushfires fuelled by climate change have killed at least 29 people and devastated vast swathes of the country since September . <p> Those blazes have been followed by extreme weather -- including intense storms that have battered parts of Australia with giant hail , floods and landslides -- that has hampered clean-up operations in some areas . <p> Heavy rainfall has helped to contain and , in some cases , extinguish long-running blazes but an expected return to searing heat and gusting winds Thursday was flagged as a cause for concern as dozens of fires continued to burn . <p> Temperatures will tip above 40 degrees Celsius ( 104 degrees Fahrenheit ) in parts of the country -- including in Australia 's biggest city @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ kilometres per hour ( 55 miles per hour ) are forecast in some areas . <p> New South Wales Rural Fire Service commissioner Shane Fitzsimmons said firefighters were on " heightened levels of alert " , with bans on lighting fires in place across much of the state amid predictions of " severe " fire danger . <p> " There 's every potential for flare-ups and new ignitions to come out of some of these fire grounds , remembering we 've got thousands of kilometres of fire perimeter , " he told reporters . <p> " A lot of it has been extinguished , a lot of it is under control -- but there are still pockets that are burning and smouldering and we do n't know how many additional roots , tree stumps or branches are likely to flare up under those conditions . " <p> Cooler weather was forecast to return on Friday , again easing the immediate threat from fires , but the bushfire season still has weeks left to run . <p> The months-long crisis has sparked renewed calls for Australia 's conservative government to take @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Prime Minister Scott Morrison to reduce the country 's reliance on coal . <p> A major coal conference in Wollongong -- situated in a coal-producing region south of Sydney that has been hit by massive blazes -- was cancelled this week in response to what local climate activists said was " planned mass protest " . <p> And a January poll released this week found the environment has soared to the top of Australians ' concerns , with 41 percent of people polled by market research firm Ipsos ranking it as the most important national issue for the first time . <p> While Morrison has now reluctantly acknowledged the link between the bushfire disaster and a warming planet , he has stopped short of announcing any sweeping new measures to address to cut emissions . <p> Instead , the prime minister has signalled that his government would focus on climate adaption and building resilience. -- AFP 
@@41677415 <h> How will the Australian bushfires psychologically affect the survivors and us ? <p> 24 Jan 2020 / 10:54 H. <p> Pix for representational purpose only . <p> Hew Gill <p> Professor of Psychology &amp; Associate Provost <p> Sunway University <p> The eyes of the world are fixed on the bushfires ravaging Australia and in Malaysia we are following the progress of the 60 firefighting , rescue and recovery personnel who have joined teams from other Commonwealth nations . They have a truly huge task facing them as they confront an immense and seemingly unstoppable natural disaster that is devouring whole landscapes from skyline to horizon . Approximately 18 million acres have been completely devastated , and to give you some idea of what that actually means it is an area bigger than Pahang , Perak , Kuala Lumpur , Selangor and Negeri Sembilan that has been reduced to smoking ashes . Nearly 30 people have died , 2000 homes have been incinerated and a quarter of a million residents have been forced to flee the raging flames . The impact on the environment is almost impossible to comprehend and it is estimated that up to a billion animals @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ caused by the loss of their habitats . Many scientists are predicting that the Australian bush and its wildlife will not recover in our lifetimes , but what of the residents and fire crews , will there be any long-term effects on them ? <p> Every single person who has been forced to flee their home will have faced the difficult decision of whether to stay or to go . As dispassionate outsiders we may think it is an easy choice to make because if your life is at potential risk who would not chose safety ? But it 's a much more difficult decision than whether or not simply to leave , it 's a series of very complex and uncertain judgements . No matter how bad the fires may seem , most of us would hold onto the belief that we might be lucky , that the fire might take a different direction , that it might rain , that help might arrive or for any other number of reasons our house might survive . Even if it seems certain that the fire will come would you abandon @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ they are there when the fire arrives there is the possibility , no matter how small , that they might be able to do something to preserve the property , but once they leave they will have no control over events and a tiny fire that could have been extinguished could be enough to turn the building in a smoking shell . Then there is the question of security , if thousands of homes are unoccupied and the emergency services are busy there is always the chance that some unscrupulous people will try to take advantage of the situation , so even if a house is untouched by fire it may be robbed by thieves . You can imagine the pressure you would feel trying to make up your mind . And then , having taken one of the toughest decisions of your life you must prepare to leave which means an even harder set of choices -- what would you take with you ? Or to put it much more brutally , what would you abandon to the flames ? Your house contains all the things you have gathered @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ is the store of your treasure and your memories , so what would you leave behind ? <p> Thousands of Australians have had to answer these most difficult questions and have suffered the stress of making these awful choices , but there may be even worse to come . The research evidence overwhelmingly shows that when we are under pressure our capacity to make rational choices is impaired and this means that we make bad decisions . Being given a short time to quit your home is about as stressful as it gets and that means it is fantastically difficult to remember what is important before we even decide whether or not to leave it behind . This means that even as the immediate stress of packing and departing begins to subside the first signs of longer-term psychological trauma may begin to emerge as each victim runs the choices through their minds and questions whether or not they did the right thing . Then , in the quiet time when they are trying to sleep they may encounter perhaps the most painful experience of all : remembering something important that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ for safety in a cupboard , the family photo albums , the expensive anniversary gift , jewellery or ornament . These things are irreplaceable so the stress of loss will be greatly magnified by the feelings of foolishness or remorse , and these unhappy emotions will continue long after the smoke has cleared . <p> Thankfully most of those who have been forced to leave their homes have made it to safety , though many have suffered the shock and fear of the near miss as the bushfires have speeded ahead of them . In the current situation it is a cruel irony that towns and villages tend to be built on slopes or hill tops to avoid flooding , yet a slope can mean that a bushfire moves at 30 plus miles an hour or even faster in a strong breeze . Many householders who wait to the last moment to leave have failed to realise just how quickly their escape route may be blocked by flames . This unexpected and most dangerous aspect of the fires has almost certainly been the cause of some fatalities . For those @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ brought by loss of family or friends and this will depress any sense of relief that comes from making it to safety . Those who have lost loved ones will also suffer the agonising reflections upon what they might have done differently and whether they might have helped or saved someone . All human societies have evolved ceremonies and social rituals to deal with grief , but while the fires continue there must be other priorities and for many this means they will be prevented from expressing these very deep emotions associated with loss . Pain denied can often turn inward and as loss is often accompanied by guilt many survivors will blame themselves for things that were not really their fault . This is why easy access to counselling and support services is so important and why the Australians have placed such a heavy emphasis on deploying health professionals to all of the refugee centres . <p> We all appreciate how stressful it must be to flee from the flames and this should make us even more aware of the deep courage it needs to walk into the fire @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ beat back the inferno are displaying bravery of the very highest order through the most amazing self-control and discipline . There are few things more frightening than fire so to willingly confront the full fury of a furnace that has no limits or constraints takes a very special kind of person . However , even the most heroic firefighter is still a human and the fearlessness of today may have psychological consequences tomorrow . Some psychologists view courage almost like a ginger root -- you can keeping cutting away at it , but providing you leave some behind it will grow back again . This means that resilient people can often recover from the most demanding of circumstances , but it also means that they do not have an infinite capacity and that they need time to recuperate . The more bravery that is consumed the longer it will take to regenerate , and if it is not given sufficient time then eventually it will be all used up . People who reach this limit of their endurance may suffer a complete emotional collapse , but ginger is tangy to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ detect the signs that such a collapse is imminent . The pressures of constant stress and fatigue as days drag into weeks can significantly increase the rate at which people begin to lose their robustness so those who usually appear to be calm and collected in the most trying of circumstances may suddenly find it impossible to deal with even small shocks . This is why it is so important to monitor the mental health of all those engaged in fighting these terrible fires because increasing pressure leads to tiredness which in turn leads to mistakes which may in turn cause more pressure or even serious accidents . The Australian authorities will be aware of these concerns and are undoubtedly monitoring the situation . However , it can be very difficult to assess psychological health in a social group that is likely to place a high value on appearing to be tough . Paradoxically , those who are the most resilient of all may find it most difficult to admit that they are suffering and that they need help , so it takes the most skilled mental health professionals to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ will soon begin to subside and people will be able to return home , but for the survivors it will take many years to return to any sense of normality . How would you feel if you went back home only to find a pile of cinders and blackened rubble ? For many the shock of seeing the destruction will lead to immediate pain that then develops into depression . The evidence shows that for many people the initial elation at surviving a natural disaster is soon replaced by deep unhappiness as they realise the enormity of what has been lost . Some become almost paralysed by a sense of hopelessness at the difficulty of rebuilding and recovering from the disaster , while others throw themselves into the work of reconstruction . All will want to share their experiences and it is important that communities come together to make sense of what has happened to them and to draw upon their collective strength as they try to remake that which was lost . In psychological terms it is often best if survivors can see their tragedy as an opportunity to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ have a deep longing for what has gone . Even those who appear to have coped well with the tragedy may be more emotional or volatile than usual . Many will express their psychological discomfort by being easily upset or becoming angry at the slightest provocation ; many will want to seek security in physical contact through hugs , kisses , or simply being close to others . In the short to medium term the drive to clear up and rebuild will be a welcome distraction , and all the busyness will mean that many people appear to be fine . However , even the toughest may be psychologically fragile for many months or years to come and in many cases the stresses of the emergency may only emerge in the future . Nowadays we are all much more aware of post-traumatic stress disorder ( PTSD ) , but many sufferers find it difficult to admit that they have a problem , especially if the nightmares , flashbacks and anxiety attacks only manifest themselves months or even years after the events . PTSD can be a silent epidemic , almost @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ unless treated . All of the communities that have been affected by this disaster will require access to good mental health provision for a very long time to come , and every member of these communities will need to be alert for the signs of PTSD in the people they know so that treatment can be speedily offered . <p> What is true of the survivors will also be true of the firefighters . When the fires have been conquered many of those who have taken part will justifiable experience a great elation and joy at a job well done . However , this happiness may quickly be replaced by a sense of anti-climax , almost a feeling of emptiness ; boredom may shade into depression , and there will also be the ever-present possibility of PTSD . People who have been in dangerous situations will often reflect upon the experience and think through how they might respond if confronted with similar dangers in the future . This is a natural and healthy thing to do because it helps us prepare and be ready for similar challenges should they arise @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and difficult to control , particularly as people start to become anxious about all of the things that could have gone wrong . We all fervently hope that the 60 brave Malaysians will return unharmed when the crisis is past , but there is always the possibility that they will need help and support to address mental health issues that may arise for a long time in the future . <p> When the fires have finally burnt themselves out the bush will begin to recover quickly and very soon the green shoots of new growth will emerge . There will also come a day when the reconstruction is completed and what appears to be normality returns to all of the towns and villages that have been ravaged . However , even for those who have cheerfully weathered the firestorm there may be psychological challenges yet to come . Every resident of a property that has been destroyed will be anxious that it could happen again . Home is the one place where we all want to feel completely safe , so the worry that a similar conflagration could happen again @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to continue their lives in the same place and they will leave . This may cause some communities to shrink and to turn inward on themselves . Some communities may develop clear fault-lines as survivors who leave are replaced by new faces that will never be able to understand what their neighbours have experienced . The survivors who do not return will most probably move to bigger towns or cities where the emergency was experienced by their new neighbours as haze and a black smudge on the far horizon . Those who did not directly experience the tragedy will forget and those among them who were there may feel isolated and alone . The psychological scars for many may never heal . <p> There is one last and significant point that we need to consider and it is one that may benefit all of us , not just kami but kita . At a time when the green bush of Australia is incinerated to grey ash , the canals of Venice run dry and the Malaysian monsoon brings unpredictable flash floods it is clear that the climate is changing . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ climate change has played a major part in all these events and that the situation may get much worse unless we humans do something to stop climate change getting worse . One of the most powerful ways to help people heal psychologically is to ensure that whatever inflicted the original damage can not happen again . Each of us has the choice to take action to reduce climate change by taking the actions that together will combine to help the World achieve the United Nations Sustainability Goals . The future is in your hands and now you know you have the power to prevent physical and psychological harm what is stopping you ?
